					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"><span class="mw-redirectedfrom">(Redirected from <a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Jude&amp;redirect=no" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Jude">Saint Jude</a>)</span></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">One of the Twelve Apostles of Jesus</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"St. Jude" redirects here. For other saints, see <a href="/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus" title="Jude, brother of Jesus">Jude, brother of Jesus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/St._Jude_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="St. Jude (disambiguation)">St. Jude (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Thaddaeus" redirects here. For the saint known as Mar Addai, see <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus_of_Edessa" class="mw-redirect" title="Thaddeus of Edessa">Thaddeus of Edessa</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus" title="Thaddeus">Thaddeus</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>

AD 65<br /><a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Armenia_(antiquity)" title="Kingdom of Armenia (antiquity)">Armenia</a><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data">All <a href="/wiki/Christian_denomination" title="Christian denomination">Christian denominations</a> that venerate <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saints</a> <br /></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Pre-Congregation" class="mw-redirect" title="Pre-Congregation">Pre-Congregation</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/St._Thaddeus_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Thaddeus Monastery">St. Thaddeus Armenian Monastery</a>, northern Iran; <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">Saint Peter's, Rome</a>; <a href="/wiki/Reims" title="Reims">Reims</a>, <a href="/wiki/Toulouse" title="Toulouse">Toulouse</a>, France<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. lost causes; desperate situations; hospitals; <a href="/wiki/St._Petersburg,_Florida" title="St. Petersburg, Florida">St. Petersburg, Florida</a>; Cotta;<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Chicago_Police_Department" title="Chicago Police Department">Chicago Police Department</a>; <a href="/wiki/Clube_de_Regatas_do_Flamengo" class="mw-redirect" title="Clube de Regatas do Flamengo">Clube de Regatas do Flamengo</a> (in <a href="/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro,_Brazil" class="mw-redirect" title="Rio de Janeiro, Brazil">Rio de Janeiro, Brazil</a>); places in the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a> (<a href="/wiki/Lucena,_Philippines" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucena, Philippines">Lucena</a> in <a href="/wiki/Quezon_(province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Quezon (province)">Quezon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sibalom" title="Sibalom">Sibalom</a> in <a href="/wiki/Antique_(province)" title="Antique (province)">Antique</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Trece_Martires" title="Trece Martires">Trece Martires</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cavite" title="Cavite">Cavite</a>); and <a href="/wiki/Sinajana" class="mw-redirect" title="Sinajana">Sinajana</a> (in <a href="/wiki/Guam" title="Guam">Guam</a>)</td></tr></tbody></table><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1092331828">@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .contains-special-characters{width:22em}}</style><div class="side-box metadata side-box-right contains-special-characters noprint selfref"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style>
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/CopticLetters.svg/40px-CopticLetters.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="33" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/CopticLetters.svg/60px-CopticLetters.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/CopticLetters.svg/80px-CopticLetters.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="475" data-file-height="394" /></div>
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><b>This article contains <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> text.</b> Without proper <a href="/wiki/Help:Multilingual_support#Coptic" title="Help:Multilingual support">rendering support</a>, you may see <a href="/wiki/Specials_(Unicode_block)#Replacement_character" title="Specials (Unicode block)">question marks, boxes, or other symbols</a>&#32;instead of Coptic letters.</div></div>
</div>
<p><b>Jude</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἰούδας Ἰακώβου</span></span> translit. Ioúdas Iakóbou) was one of the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">Twelve Apostles</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>. He is generally identified as <b>Thaddeus</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Θαδδαῖος</span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>: <span lang="cop">ⲑⲁⲇⲇⲉⲟⲥ</span>; <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac/Aramaic</a>: ܝܗܘܕܐ ܫܠܝܚܐ<sup id="cite_ref-:0_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>), and is also variously called <b>Judas Thaddaeus</b>, <b>Jude Thaddaeus</b>, <b>Jude of James</b>, or <b>Lebbaeus</b><sup id="cite_ref-:1_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and is considered as the founding father and the first <a href="/wiki/Catholicos-Patriarch" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicos-Patriarch">Catholicos-Patriarch</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church" title="Armenian Apostolic Church">Armenian Apostolic Church</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He is sometimes identified with <a href="/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus" title="Jude, brother of Jesus">Jude, the brother of Jesus</a>, but is clearly distinguished from <a href="/wiki/Judas_Iscariot" title="Judas Iscariot">Judas Iscariot</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostle</a> who betrayed Jesus prior to his crucifixion. Catholic writer Michal Hunt suggests that Judas Thaddaeus became known as Jude after early translators of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> from <a href="/wiki/Koine_Greek" title="Koine Greek">Greek</a> into English sought to distinguish him from Judas Iscariot and subsequently abbreviated his forename.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Most versions of the New Testament in languages other than English and <a href="/wiki/French_translations_of_the_Bible" class="mw-redirect" title="French translations of the Bible">French</a> refer to Judas and Jude by the same name.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church" title="Armenian Apostolic Church">Armenian Apostolic Church</a> honors Thaddeus along with <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew_the_Apostle" title="Bartholomew the Apostle">Saint Bartholomew</a> as its <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>, he is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of desperate cases and lost causes.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Saint Jude's attribute is a <a href="/wiki/Club_(weapon)" title="Club (weapon)">club</a>. He is also often shown in <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icons</a> with a flame around his head. This represents his presence at <a href="/wiki/Pentecost" title="Pentecost">Pentecost</a>, when he received the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> with the other apostles. Another common attribute is Jude holding an image of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesus Christ">Jesus Christ</a>, known as <a href="/wiki/Image_of_Edessa" title="Image of Edessa">the Image of Edessa</a>. In some instances, he may be shown with a scroll or a book (the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>) or holding a carpenter's rule.<sup id="cite_ref-DomesticChurch_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DomesticChurch-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena,_Italy.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/220px-Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/330px-Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/440px-Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="862" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Jude_Thaddaeus_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena,_Italy.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Jude the Apostle, detail of the mosaic in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Vitale" title="Basilica of San Vitale">Basilica of San Vitale</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ravenna" title="Ravenna">Ravenna</a>, 6th century</div></div></div>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Identity">Identity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Identity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_Testament">New Testament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: New Testament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Jude is clearly distinguished from <a href="/wiki/Judas_Iscariot" title="Judas Iscariot">Judas Iscariot</a>, another apostle and later the betrayer of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a>. Both Jude and Judas are translations of the name Ὶούδας in the <a href="/wiki/Koine_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Koine Greek language">Koine Greek language</a> original text of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, which in turn is a Greek variant of <a href="/wiki/Judah_(given_name)" title="Judah (given name)">Judah</a> (<i>Y'hudah</i>), a name which was common among Jews at the time. In most Bibles in languages other than English and French, Jude and Judas are referred to by the same name.
</p><p>Aside from Judas Iscariot, the New Testament mentions Jude or Judas six times, in four different contexts:
</p>
<ol><li>"Jude of James", one of the twelve apostles (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#6:16" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 6:16</a> and <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Acts#1:13" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Acts">Acts 1:13</a>);</li>
<li>"Judas, (not Judas Iscariot)", apparently an apostle (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/John#14:22" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/John">John 14:22</a>);</li>
<li>the <a href="/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus" title="Jude, brother of Jesus">brother of Jesus</a> (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#13:55" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 13:55</a>, <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Mark#6:3" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Mark">Mark 6:3</a>);</li>
<li>the writer of the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>, who identifies himself as "the brother of James" (<a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Jude#1:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Jude">Jude 1</a>).</li></ol>
<p>Catholic tradition generally holds all four to be the same person,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="Catholic tradition is normally available online, please provide resource (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> while Protestants generally believe 1 and 2 to be one person,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> although theologian <a href="/wiki/Raymond_E._Brown" title="Raymond E. Brown">Raymond Brown</a> saw the identification as uncertain.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The latter two are also usually thought to be the same person, although this too is not certain, but different from 1 and 2.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Brother_of_James_or_son_of_James.3F"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Brother_of_James_or_son_of_James?">Brother of James or son of James?</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Brother of James or son of James?">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Translations into English from the original Greek of the New Testament vary in their rendering of Luke 6:16 and Acts 1:13. A literal translation of the references to Jude in these passages gives "Jude of James" (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἰούδας Ἰακώβου</span></span>), as in <a href="/wiki/Young%27s_Literal_Translation" title="Young&#39;s Literal Translation">Young's Literal Translation</a> of the Bible, but scholars differ on whether this means "Jude, brother of James" or "Jude, son of James". The King James and the <a href="/wiki/Douay%E2%80%93Rheims_Bible" title="Douay–Rheims Bible">Douay-Rheims</a> versions call him  "Judas the brother of James", making him the same person as the writer of the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>, who identifies himself as "Jude, the servant of Jesus Christ, and brother of James" (Jude 1:1).
</p><p>Most modern translations (including the <a href="/wiki/New_International_Version" title="New International Version">New International Version</a>, <a href="/wiki/Revised_Standard_Version" title="Revised Standard Version">Revised Standard Version</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Revised_Standard_Version" title="New Revised Standard Version">New Revised Standard Version</a>), identify him as "Jude the son of James", and not the same person as the author of the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>. Protestant scholar <a href="/wiki/Darrell_L._Bock" title="Darrell L. Bock">Darrell L. Bock</a> writes that it must mean "son" not "brother", because when "brother" is intended, the Greek word for "brother" (adelphos) is present.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Bock also says that means he was not the brother of Jesus. Additionally the use of the genitive case of "James" (Iakovou) in Greek, usually signifies or implies the person's father to be distinguished from his homonyms.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Brother_of_Jesus.3F"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Brother_of_Jesus?">Brother of Jesus?</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Brother of Jesus?">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Opinion is divided on whether Jude the apostle was also <a href="/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus" title="Jude, brother of Jesus">Jude, brother of Jesus</a>, the traditional author of the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Generally, Catholics believe the two Judes are the same person,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> while Protestants do not.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="Reliable source needed (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>According to the surviving fragments of the work <i>Exposition of the Sayings of the Lord</i> of the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Father" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic Father">Apostolic Father</a> <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias of Hierapolis</a>, who lived c.&#160;70–163 AD, Mary the wife of Cleophas or Alphaeus would be the mother of <a href="/wiki/Jude_(brother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Jude (brother of Jesus)">Judas the brother of Jesus</a> that Papias identifies with Thaddeus:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Mary the wife of Cleophas or Alphaeus, who was the mother of James the bishop and apostle, and of Simon and Thaddeus, and of one Joseph...<i>(Fragment X)</i><sup id="cite_ref-papias_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-papias-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Possible_identity_with_Thaddeus">Possible identity with Thaddeus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Possible identity with Thaddeus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Thaddeus,_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d4/St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg/220px-St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d4/St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg/330px-St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/d4/St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg/440px-St._Thaddeus%2C_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2500" data-file-height="1990" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Thaddeus,_St._Sandukht_and_other_Christians_in_Sanatruk%27s_prison.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>St. Thaddeus, St. Sandukht and other Christians in <a href="/wiki/Sanatruk" title="Sanatruk">Sanatruk</a>'s prison</div></div></div>
<p>In the apostolic lists at <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#10:3" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 10:3</a> and <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Mark#3:18" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Mark">Mark 3:18</a>, Jude is omitted, but there is a Thaddeus (or in some manuscripts of Matthew 10:3, "Lebbaeus who was surnamed Thaddaeus", as in the <a href="/wiki/King_James_Version" title="King James Version">King James Version</a>) listed in his place. This has led many Christians since early times to harmonize the lists by positing a "Jude Thaddeus", known by either name. This is made plausible by the fact that "Thaddeus" seems to be a nickname (see <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus" title="Thaddeus">Thaddeus</a>) and that many New Testament figures have multiple names (such as <a href="/wiki/St._Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter">Simon Peter</a> and <a href="/wiki/Barnabas" title="Barnabas">Joseph Barnabas</a>). A further reason is the fact that the name "Judas" was tarnished by <a href="/wiki/Judas_Iscariot" title="Judas Iscariot">Judas Iscariot</a>. It has been argued that for this reason, it is unsurprising that Mark and Matthew refer to him by an alternate name.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some <a href="/wiki/Biblical_scholar" class="mw-redirect" title="Biblical scholar">Biblical scholars</a> reject this theory, however, holding that Jude and Thaddeus did not represent the same person.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> They have proposed alternative theories to explain the discrepancy: an unrecorded replacement of one for the other during the ministry of Jesus because of <a href="/wiki/Apostasy" title="Apostasy">apostasy</a> or death;<sup id="cite_ref-AMJ_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AMJ-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> the possibility that "twelve" was a symbolic number and an estimation;<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> or simply that the names were not recorded perfectly by the early church.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thaddeus, one of the twelve apostles, is often indistinguishable from <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus_of_Edessa" class="mw-redirect" title="Thaddeus of Edessa">Thaddeus of Edessa</a>, one of the <a href="/wiki/Seventy_Disciples" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventy Disciples">Seventy Disciples</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In some Latin manuscripts of Matthew 10:3, Thaddeus is called <a href="/wiki/Judas_the_Zealot" title="Judas the Zealot">Judas the Zealot</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_other_manuscripts">In other manuscripts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: In other manuscripts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/The_Golden_Legend" class="mw-redirect" title="The Golden Legend">the Golden Legend</a>, which is a collection of <a href="/wiki/Hagiographies" class="mw-redirect" title="Hagiographies">hagiographies</a> compiled by <a href="/wiki/Jacobus_de_Voragine" title="Jacobus de Voragine">Jacobus de Voragine</a> in the thirteenth century:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>This Judas was called by many names. He was said Judas of James, for he was brother to James the Less, and he was called Thaddeus, which is as much to say as taking a prince; or Thadee is said of Thadea, that is a vesture, and of Deus, that is God, for he was vesture royal of God by ornament of virtues, by which he took Christ the prince. He is said also in the History Ecclesiastic, Lebbæus, which is as much to say as heart, or worshipper of heart. Or he is said Lebbæus of lebes, that is a vessel of heart by great hardiness, or a worshipper of heart by purity, a vessel by plenitude of grace, for he deserved to be a vessel of virtues and a caldron of grace.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p> The same work writes that "<a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Canaanite" class="mw-redirect" title="Simon the Canaanite">Simon Cananean</a> and Judas Thaddeus were brethren of <a href="/wiki/James_the_Less" title="James the Less">James the Less</a> and sons of <a href="/wiki/Mary_Cleophas" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary Cleophas">Mary Cleophas</a>, who was married to <a href="/wiki/Alphaeus" title="Alphaeus">Alpheus</a>."
</p><p>The Letter or Epistle of Saint Jude in the Bible is usually attributed to the Apostle Jude, and is a short piece. Some statues of Saint Jude include the letter (such as the statue of Saint Jude by Adam Kossowski in Faversham, Kent).<sup id="cite_ref-Shrine_of_Saint_Jude_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shrine_of_Saint_Jude-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Tradition_and_legend">Tradition and legend</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Tradition and legend">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg/220px-Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg/330px-Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c9/Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg/440px-Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="514" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Thaddeus Monastery">Saint Thaddeus Armenian Monastery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Iran</a></div></div></div>
<p>Tradition holds that Saint Jude preached the Gospel in <a href="/wiki/Judea" title="Judea">Judea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Samaria" title="Samaria">Samaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Idumaea" class="mw-redirect" title="Idumaea">Idumaea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He is also said to have visited <a href="/wiki/Beirut" title="Beirut">Beirut</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edessa,_Mesopotamia" class="mw-redirect" title="Edessa, Mesopotamia">Edessa</a>, though the emissary of the latter mission is also identified as <a href="/wiki/Thaddeus_of_Edessa" class="mw-redirect" title="Thaddeus of Edessa">Thaddeus of Edessa</a>, Addai,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> one of the Seventy.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The 14th-century writer <a href="/wiki/Nicephorus_Callistus_Xanthopoulos" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos">Nicephorus Callistus</a> makes Jude the bridegroom at the <a href="/wiki/Wedding_at_Cana" title="Wedding at Cana">wedding at Cana</a>. The legend reports that St. Jude was born into a <a href="/wiki/Jew" class="mw-redirect" title="Jew">Jewish</a> family in <a href="/wiki/Paneas" class="mw-redirect" title="Paneas">Paneas</a>, a town in <a href="/wiki/Galilee" title="Galilee">Galilee</a> later rebuilt during the Roman period and renamed <a href="/wiki/Caesarea_Philippi" class="mw-redirect" title="Caesarea Philippi">Caesarea Philippi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MajerníkPonessa2005_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MajerníkPonessa2005-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Wilson2004_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilson2004-32">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In all probability, he spoke both <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Aramaic language">Aramaic</a>, like almost all of his contemporaries in that area, and was a farmer by trade.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> According to the legend, St. Jude was son of <a href="/wiki/Clopas" title="Clopas">Clopas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Clopas" title="Mary of Clopas">Mary of Clopas</a>, sister of the <a href="/wiki/Mary,_the_mother_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary, the mother of Jesus">Virgin Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Tradition has it that Jude's father, Clopas, was martyred because of his forthright and outspoken devotion to the risen Christ.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Although <a href="/wiki/Saint_Gregory_the_Illuminator" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Gregory the Illuminator">Saint Gregory the Illuminator</a> is credited as the "Apostle to the Armenians", when he baptized King <a href="/wiki/Tiridates_III_of_Armenia" title="Tiridates III of Armenia">Tiridates III of Armenia</a> in 301, converting the Armenians, the Apostles Jude and <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew" class="mw-redirect" title="Bartholomew">Bartholomew</a> are traditionally believed to have been the first to bring <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> to <a href="/wiki/History_of_Armenia" title="History of Armenia">Armenia</a>, and are therefore venerated as the patron saints of the <a href="/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church" title="Armenian Apostolic Church">Armenian Apostolic Church</a>. Linked to this tradition is the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Thaddeus_Monastery" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Thaddeus Monastery">Saint Thaddeus Monastery</a> (now in northern Iran) and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Bartholomew_Monastery" title="Saint Bartholomew Monastery">Saint Bartholomew Monastery</a> (now in southeastern Turkey) which were both constructed in what was then Armenia.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg/220px-Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg/330px-Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg/440px-Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="6000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Tadeos_Glxavor.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Saint_Thaddeus_Church,_Masis" title="Saint Thaddeus Church, Masis">Saint Thaddeus Church, Masis</a></div></div></div><p> On October 4, 2015, <a href="/wiki/Karekin_II" title="Karekin II">the Catholicos of All Armenians Karekin II</a>, consecrated the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Thaddeus_Church,_Masis" title="Saint Thaddeus Church, Masis">Saint Thaddeus Church</a> in Masis, <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a>. The construction site of the Church was chosen by <a href="/wiki/Vazgen_I" title="Vazgen I">Vazgen I</a>, the Catholicos of All Armenians, in 1991 and the ground blessing service was conducted in the same year.
</p><h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_remains">Death and remains</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Death and remains">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg/220px-Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg/330px-Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg/440px-Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="893" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Thaddeus_mosaic.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Symbol of his <a href="/wiki/Martyrdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Martyrdom">martyrdom</a></div></div></div>
<p>According to tradition, Saint Jude suffered <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyrdom</a> about 65 AD in <a href="/wiki/Beirut" title="Beirut">Beirut</a>, in the Roman province of <a href="/wiki/Syria_(Roman_province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Syria (Roman province)">Syria</a> during the <a href="/wiki/1st_century_in_Lebanon" title="1st century in Lebanon">1st century in Lebanon</a> together with the apostle <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Simon the Zealot</a>, with whom he is usually connected. The axe that he is often shown holding in pictures symbolizes the way in which he was killed.<sup id="cite_ref-jude_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jude-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Their acts and martyrdom were recorded in an <i>Acts of Simon and Jude</i> that was among the collection of passions and legends traditionally associated with the legendary <a href="/wiki/Abdias_of_Babylon" title="Abdias of Babylon">Abdias, bishop of Babylon</a>, and said to have been translated into Latin by his disciple Tropaeus Africanus, according to the <i><a href="/wiki/Golden_Legend" title="Golden Legend">Golden Legend</a></i> account of the saints.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to one account, Saint Jude's body was brought from Beirut to Rome and placed in a crypt in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a> which is visited by many devotees. His bones are in the left transept of St. Peter's Basilica under the main altar of St. Joseph, in one tomb with the remains of the apostle Simon the Zealot. These were moved here on 27 December 1665.<sup id="cite_ref-Shrine_of_Saint_Jude_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shrine_of_Saint_Jude-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> According to another popular tradition, the remains of St. Jude were preserved in an Armenian monastery on an island in the northern part of <a href="/wiki/Issyk-Kul_Lake" class="mw-redirect" title="Issyk-Kul Lake">Issyk-Kul Lake</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyzstan" title="Kyrgyzstan">Kyrgyzstan</a> at least until the mid-15th century. Later legends either deny that the remains are preserved there or claim that they were moved to a yet more desolate stronghold in the <a href="/wiki/Pamir_Mountains" title="Pamir Mountains">Pamir Mountains</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>A plain <a href="/wiki/Ossuary" title="Ossuary">ossuary</a> marked with the inscription "Judas Thaddaeus" (<i>Ιουδας Θαδδαιου</i>) was found in <a href="/wiki/Kefar_Barukh" class="mw-redirect" title="Kefar Barukh">Kefar Barukh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jezreel_Valley" title="Jezreel Valley">Jezreel Valley</a>, alongside fragments of four uninscribed ossuaries. The site was dated by lamps and other pottery to no later than the early second century.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg/220px-Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg/330px-Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg/440px-Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1427" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_of_Saints_Simon_and_Jude_Thaddeus_in_Rudno.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Church of <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Saints Simon</a> and Jude Thaddeus in <a href="/wiki/Rudno,_Lubusz_Voivodeship" title="Rudno, Lubusz Voivodeship">Rudno</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a></div></div></div>
<p>Jude is traditionally depicted carrying the image of Jesus in his hand or close to his chest, betokening the legend of the <a href="/wiki/Image_of_Edessa" title="Image of Edessa">Image of Edessa</a>, recorded in apocryphal correspondence between Jesus and <a href="/wiki/Abgar" class="mw-redirect" title="Abgar">Abgar</a> which is reproduced in <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius</a>' <i>History Ecclesiastica, I, xiii</i>. Eusebius relates that King <a href="/wiki/Abgarus_of_Edessa" class="mw-redirect" title="Abgarus of Edessa">Abgar</a> of <a href="/wiki/Edessa,_Mesopotamia" class="mw-redirect" title="Edessa, Mesopotamia">Edessa</a> (now <a href="/wiki/%C5%9Eanl%C4%B1urfa" class="mw-redirect" title="Şanlıurfa">Şanlıurfa</a> in southeast <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>) sent a letter to Jesus seeking a cure for an illness afflicting him. With the letter he sent his envoy <i>Hannan</i>, the keeper of the archives, offering his own home city to Jesus as a safe dwelling place. The envoy painted a likeness of Jesus with choice paints (or alternatively, impressed with Abgar's faith, Jesus pressed his face into a cloth and gave it to <i>Hannan</i>) to take to Abgar with his answer. Upon seeing Jesus' image, the king placed it with great honor in one of his palatial houses. After Christ's execution, Jude Thomas the Apostle sent Addai, one of the 70 or 72 in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Luke#10:1" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Luke">Luke 10:1–12</a>, to King Abgar<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> and the king was cured. Astonished, he converted to <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>, along with many of the people under his rule. Additionally, St. Jude is often depicted with a flame above his head, representing his presence at Pentecost, when he was said to have received the Holy Spirit with the other apostles.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/220px-Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="400" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/330px-Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/440px-Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="2181" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Thaddeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of St. Jude in the <a href="/wiki/Archbasilica_of_St._John_Lateran" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbasilica of St. John Lateran">Archbasilica of St. John Lateran</a> by <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_Ottoni" title="Lorenzo Ottoni">Lorenzo Ottoni</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:SJTLima001.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/SJTLima001.JPG/220px-SJTLima001.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/SJTLima001.JPG/330px-SJTLima001.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/SJTLima001.JPG/440px-SJTLima001.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1944" data-file-height="2592" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:SJTLima001.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Procession in <a href="/wiki/Lima" title="Lima">Lima</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peru" title="Peru">Peru</a></div></div></div>
<p>According to tradition, after his martyrdom, pilgrims came to his grave to pray and many of them experienced the powerful intercessions of St. Jude. Thus the title, 'The Saint for the Hopeless and the Despaired'. St. <a href="/wiki/Bridget_of_Sweden" title="Bridget of Sweden">Bridget of Sweden</a> and St. <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard</a> had visions from God asking each to accept St. Jude as 'The Patron Saint of the Impossible'.<sup id="cite_ref-jude_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jude-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Jude is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> (with <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Simon</a>) in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/October_28" title="October 28">28 October</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is 28 October (in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a>). 
</p><p>Apostle Jude son of James (Lebbaeus, Thaddaeus) the Brother of the Lord is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">commemorated</a> on 19 June and 30 June (<a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of <a href="/wiki/12_apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="12 apostles">the Holy, Glorious and All-Praised Twelve Apostles</a>) in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:5_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Addai_of_Edessa" title="Addai of Edessa">Thaddeus of Edessa</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Apostle_of_the_Seventy" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostle of the Seventy">Apostle of the Seventy</a> (sometimes identified as Jude Thaddaeus, one of the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">Twelve Apostles</a>) is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">commemorated</a> on 21 August and 4 January (<a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Seventy_apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventy apostles">Seventy Apostles</a>) in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:4_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:5_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Preachers" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Preachers">Order of Preachers</a> (better known as the Dominicans) began working in present-day Armenia soon after their founding in 1216. At that time, there was already a substantial devotion to Saint Jude by both Catholic and Orthodox Christians in the area. This lasted until Muslim persecution drove Christians from the area in the 18th century. Devotion to Saint Jude began again in earnest in the 19th century, starting in <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, spreading to <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South America</a> and finally to the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> (starting in the vicinity of <a href="/wiki/Chicago,_Illinois" class="mw-redirect" title="Chicago, Illinois">Chicago</a>) owing to the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Claretians" title="Claretians">Claretians</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominicans</a> in the 1920s.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronage">Patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Among some Roman Catholics, Saint Jude is venerated as the "<a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of hopeless causes". This practice is said to stem from the belief that few Christians invoked him for misplaced fear of praying to Christ's betrayer, <a href="/wiki/Judas_Iscariot" title="Judas Iscariot">Judas Iscariot</a>, because of their similar names. The ignored Jude thus supposedly became quite eager to assist anyone who sought his help, to the point of interceding in the most dire of circumstances.<sup id="cite_ref-OxDictOfSaints_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OxDictOfSaints-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Saint Jude is the patron saint of the <a href="/wiki/Chicago_Police_Department" title="Chicago Police Department">Chicago Police Department</a>, of Customs Officers, of <a href="/wiki/Clube_de_Regatas_do_Flamengo" class="mw-redirect" title="Clube de Regatas do Flamengo">Clube de Regatas do Flamengo</a> (a <a href="/wiki/Football_(soccer)" class="mw-redirect" title="Football (soccer)">soccer</a> team in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil) and of two St Jude's GAA teams, the first in Templeogue Dublin 6W and also St Jude's GAA club in Southampton &amp; Bournemouth (UK).  His other patronages include desperate situations and hospitals. One of his namesakes is <a href="/wiki/St._Jude_Children%27s_Research_Hospital" title="St. Jude Children&#39;s Research Hospital">St. Jude Children's Research Hospital</a> in <a href="/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee" title="Memphis, Tennessee">Memphis, Tennessee</a>, which has helped many children with terminal illnesses and their families since its founding in 1962.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Shrines_and_churches">Shrines and churches</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Shrines and churches">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Many countries venerate the Apostle Jude and have constructed shrines or churches dedicated to his memory. Such sites include those in Australia, Brazil, Sri Lanka,<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Cuba, India, Iran, the Philippines, New Zealand, the United Kingdom, the United States and Lebanon. The <a href="/wiki/National_Shrine_of_Saint_Jude_(United_States)" title="National Shrine of Saint Jude (United States)">National Shrine of St. Jude</a> in Chicago, Illinois, was founded in 1929 by the <a href="/wiki/Claretians" title="Claretians">Claretian Missionaries</a>. The Nationwide Center of St. Jude Devotions<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Baltimore" title="Baltimore">Baltimore</a> was founded in 1917 by the <a href="/wiki/Pallottines" title="Pallottines">Pallottines</a>. The <a href="/wiki/National_Shrine_of_Saint_Jude_Thaddeus" class="mw-redirect" title="National Shrine of Saint Jude Thaddeus">National Shrine of Saint Jude Thaddeus</a> in the Philippines was erected by the <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Manila" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Manila">Archdiocese of Manila</a> in 1954 as Espíritu Santo <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Filipino" title="Chinese Filipino">Chinese</a> Parish. The Shrine holds the saint's novena liturgy every Thursday and is now under the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_the_Divine_Word" title="Society of the Divine Word">Society of the Divine Word</a> that also runs the attached <a href="/wiki/Saint_Jude_Catholic_School_(Manila)" title="Saint Jude Catholic School (Manila)">Saint Jude Catholic School</a>. The <a href="/wiki/National_Shrine_of_Saint_Jude_(England)" title="National Shrine of Saint Jude (England)">National Shrine of Saint Jude</a> at <a href="/wiki/Faversham" title="Faversham">Faversham</a> in England was founded in 1955.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> There is also a shrine of St. Jude built by the Dominicans (Order of Preachers) in Lagos, Nigeria.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Islam">In Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: In Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">quranic</a> account of the <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciples</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Jesus</a> does not include their names, numbers, or any detailed accounts of their lives. Muslim <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a>, however, more or less agrees with the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> list and says that the disciples included <a href="/wiki/Peter_in_Islam" title="Peter in Islam">Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philip_the_Apostle" title="Philip the Apostle">Philip</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Thomas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew_the_Apostle" title="Bartholomew the Apostle">Bartholomew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle" title="Matthew the Apostle">Matthew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a>, <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Zebedee" class="mw-redirect" title="James, son of Zebedee">James</a>, Jude, <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John</a> and <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Simon the Zealot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jude_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<li id="cite_note-Wilson2004-32"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Wilson2004_32-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">However, Philostorgius, the 5th-century Arian Christian historian, says in his <i>Historia Ecclesiastica</i>: "The district of Paneas was formerly called Dan. But in the course of time it came to be called Caesarea Philippi, and later still, when the heathen erected in it a statue of the God Pan, its name was changed to Paneas."<sup id="cite_ref-Wilson2004_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilson2004-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup></span>
